signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        ...\\n    }\\n}\\n```\\n\\n### Key Format Requirements\\n\\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \\n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\\n\\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \\n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \\n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\\n\\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\\n\\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \\n\\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\\n\\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \\n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \\n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\\n\\n## Python environment\\n\\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\\n\\n## Decoder Reference\\n\\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\\n- Optional arguments:\\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\\n\\n### Purpose\\n\\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\\n\\n### Consistency requirements\\n\\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\\n- Loading of data\\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\\n\\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \\n\\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \\n\\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \\n\\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \\n\\nYou will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference paper and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \\n\\n---\\n\\n## Conversion Workflow\\n\\n**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Each step has explicit completion criteria. Do not proceed to the next step until the current step is complete. Use the exact step names below when documenting progress in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n**STATUS TRACKING**: At the start of each step, update its status to `IN PROGRESS` in CONVERSION_NOTES.md. When complete, update to `COMPLETE`. This helps you and the user track progress.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 0: Setup and Initialization\\n\\n**Goal**: Set up the working environment and create the notes file.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. **FIRST**: Create `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` with the template structure shown at the end of this document\\n2. Verify you can run `python3` and import key packages (numpy, torch)\\n3. List the contents of this directory to understand what files are available\\n4. **REMINDER**: Do NOT look at any files outside this directory\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists with the proper template structure\\n- You have confirmed Python and key packages work\\n- You have listed directory contents in CONVERSION_NOTES.md\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 1, verify that `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists by running `ls -la CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. If it doesn't exist, STOP and create it now.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 1: Reference Code Exploration\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at code in the `code` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\\n\\n**Goal**: Understand how the original authors loaded and processed their data.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Look for documentation or README files in the `code` directory\\n- Read the provided reference code bases to see how the reference paper loaded and manipulated the data\\n- Understand which functions are being called to read, curate, process, and manipulate the data\\n- For neural imaging data, does delta F over F need to be computed? \\n- For electrophysiology neural data, do cells need to be filtered based on quality? \\n- Note to file important functions, their inputs, and outputs\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented key functions and their purposes in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 1\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 2, update Step 1 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 2: Dataset Exploration\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at data in the `data` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\\n\\n**Goal**: Understand the native data structure\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Identify how data is organized (all files, formats, hierarchies)\\n- Determine available variables and their meanings\\n- Check data dimensions and data types\\n- Look for documentation or README files\\n- Note the data organization and structure\\n- Note dataset size parameters such as the **total number** of subjects (mice), sessions, trials, and neurons\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented the data structure, file formats, size, and available variables in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 2\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 3, update Step 2 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 3: Reference Text Reading\\n\\n**Goal**: Extract key information from the paper and methods.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Scan the reference papers and methods.txt for important details about the data\\n- Look for and note to file:\\n  - Any information about dataset size (number of neurons, subjects, sessions, trials)\\n  - Fraction of data with different values, e.g. fraction of rewarded outcomes\\n  - Data processing, curation, and filtering\\n  - Variables important to experiments\\n  - Temporal alignment\\n  - Temporal binning\\n- Read the reference paper to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 3\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 4: Check for Consistency\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Compare your understanding of:\\n  - Important functions from exploring the reference code\\n  - Dataset structure from exploring the dataset\\n  - Analysis described in the reference texts\\n- Note and investigate any discrepancies\\n- In particular, look for discrepancies in:\\n  - Experimental and behavioral variables\\n  - Data processing\\n  - Data curation\\n- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 4\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 5: Mapping Planning\\n\\n**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Map source data elements to target format components:\\n  - Which variables contain neural activity?\\n  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\\n  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\\n  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \\n- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\\n  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\\n  - Temporal alignment of trials\\n  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\\n  - Brain regions recorded from\\n- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\\n- Check for variables indicating valid data periods \\u2014 exclude invalid data\\n- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\\n- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented all decisions under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 6: Script Development\\n\\n**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\\n- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\\n- Include options:\\n  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\\n  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\\n  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\\n- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\\n- Write efficient code:\\n  - Vectorize loops\\n  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\\n  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\\n  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\\n- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\\n  - There are no temporal misalignments\\n  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\\n- Write clear, well-documented functions\\n- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\\n- Validate data shapes and types at each step\\n- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\\n\\n**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\\n  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\\n  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\\n  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \\n  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\\n2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\\n  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \\n  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \\n  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \\n3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\\n4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\\n  a. Verify no errors reported.\\n  b. Attempt to address any warnings\\n  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- Processing plots show no anomalies\\n- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 7\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\\n2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\\n\\n**Investigate any issues**:\\n- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- Decoder training completes without errors\\n- Loss decreases over epochs\\n- Accuracies are above chance\\n- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 8\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\\n2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\\n3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\\n4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \\n5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \\n6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \\n7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\\n8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\\n9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\\n- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 9\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 10: Critical Review 1\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\\n\\n**Check 1: Output log verification**\\nRead through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \\n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \\n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \\n- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \\n- For each major processing step \\u2014 (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction \\u2014 identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \\n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \\n- Fix any mismatches. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\\n\\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \\n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \\n- Fix any discovered issues. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \\n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 10\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \\n2. **Wait for complete execution** \\u2014 this may take significant time for large datasets\\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \\n\\n**Done when**: \\n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 11\\\" with a table of accuracies\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\\n\\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \\n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \\n\\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers's accuracy for each variable\\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \\n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \\n\\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \\n\\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\\n2. Check temporal alignment \\u2014 plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \\n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 12\\\".\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\\n\\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\\n  - Key decisions and rationale\\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\\n  - Validation results and tables\\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\\n  - Dataset description\\n  - How to load and use the converted data\\n  - Output format specification\\n  - Key statistics\\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\\n\\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\\n\\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Any information about dataset size (number of neurons, subjects, sessions, trials)\\n  - Fraction of data with different values, e.g. fraction of rewarded outcomes\\n  - Data processing, curation, and filtering\\n  - Variables important to experiments\\n  - Temporal alignment\\n  - Temporal binning\\n- Read the reference paper to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 3\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 4: Check for Consistency\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Compare your understanding of:\\n  - Important functions from exploring the reference code\\n  - Dataset structure from exploring the dataset\\n  - Analysis described in the reference texts\\n- Note and investigate any discrepancies\\n- In particular, look for discrepancies in:\\n  - Experimental and behavioral variables\\n  - Data processing\\n  - Data curation\\n- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 4\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 5: Mapping Planning\\n\\n**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Map source data elements to target format components:\\n  - Which variables contain neural activity?\\n  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\\n  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\\n  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \\n- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\\n  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\\n  - Temporal alignment of trials\\n  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\\n  - Brain regions recorded from\\n- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\\n- Check for variables indicating valid data periods \\u2014 exclude invalid data\\n- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\\n- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented all decisions under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 6: Script Development\\n\\n**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\\n- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\\n- Include options:\\n  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\\n  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\\n  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\\n- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\\n- Write efficient code:\\n  - Vectorize loops\\n  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\\n  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\\n  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\\n- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\\n  - There are no temporal misalignments\\n  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\\n- Write clear, well-documented functions\\n- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\\n- Validate data shapes and types at each step\\n- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\\n\\n**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\\n  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\\n  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\\n  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \\n  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\\n2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\\n  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \\n  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \\n  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \\n3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\\n4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\\n  a. Verify no errors reported.\\n  b. Attempt to address any warnings\\n  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- Processing plots show no anomalies\\n- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 7\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\\n2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\\n\\n**Investigate any issues**:\\n- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- Decoder training completes without errors\\n- Loss decreases over epochs\\n- Accuracies are above chance\\n- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 8\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\\n2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\\n3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\\n4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \\n5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \\n6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \\n7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\\n8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\\n9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\\n- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 9\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 10: Critical Review 1\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\\n\\n**Check 1: Output log verification**\\nRead through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \\n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \\n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \\n- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \\n- For each major processing step \\u2014 (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction \\u2014 identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \\n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \\n- Fix any mismatches. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\\n\\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \\n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \\n- Fix any discovered issues. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \\n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 10\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \\n2. **Wait for complete execution** \\u2014 this may take significant time for large datasets\\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \\n\\n**Done when**: \\n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 11\\\" with a table of accuracies\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\\n\\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \\n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \\n\\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers's accuracy for each variable\\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \\n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \\n\\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \\n\\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\\n2. Check temporal alignment \\u2014 plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \\n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 12\\\".\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\\n\\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\\n  - Key decisions and rationale\\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\\n  - Validation results and tables\\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\\n  - Dataset description\\n  - How to load and use the converted data\\n  - Output format specification\\n  - Key statistics\\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\\n\\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\\n\\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. The wheel position\n        is first interpolated to a uniform sampling rate. Then velocity and acceleration are computed, during which\n        a Butterworth low-pass filter is applied.\n\n        Parameters\n        ----------\n        fs: int, float\n            Sampling frequency for the wheel position, default is 1000 Hz\n        corner_frequency: int, float\n            Corner frequency of Butterworth low-pass filter, default is 20\n        order: int, float\n            Order of Butterworth low_pass filter, default is 8\n        collection: str\n            Alf collection of wheel data\n        \"\"\"\n        if not collection:\n            collection = self._find_behaviour_collection('wheel')\n        wheel_raw = self.one.load_object(self.eid, 'wheel', collection=collection, revision=self.revision or None)\n        if wheel_raw['position'].shape[0] != wheel_raw['timestamps'].shape[0]:\n            raise ValueError(\"Length mismatch between 'wheel.position' and 'wheel.timestamps\")\n        # resample the wheel position and compute velocity, acceleration\n        self.wheel = pd.DataFrame(columns=['times', 'position', 'velocity', 'acceleration'])\n        self.wheel['position'], self.wheel['times'] = interpolate_position(\n            wheel_raw['timestamps'], wheel_raw['position'], freq=fs)\n        self.wheel['velocity'], self.wheel['acceleration'] = velocity_filtered(\n            self.wheel['position'], fs=fs, corner_frequency=corner_frequency, order=order)\n        self.wheel = self.wheel.apply(np.float32)\n        self.data_info.loc[self.data_info['name'] == 'wheel', 'is_loaded'] = True\n\n    def load_pose(self, likelihood_thr=0.9, views=['left', 'right', 'body'], tracker='dlc'):\n        \"\"\"\n        Function to load the pose estimation results (DLC) into SessionLoader.pose. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The AI applies several filters via `create_trials_mask()`:\n- Reaction time (firstMovement_times - stimOn_times) must be between 0.08s and 2.0s\n- Trial length (feedback_times - goCue_times) must be <= 10.0s\n- No NaN in: stimOn_times, choice, feedback_times, probabilityLeft, firstMovement_times, feedbackType\n- Exclude no-choice trials (choice == 0)\n- Additionally, trials must have valid wheel and whisker motion energy data (at least 2 non-NaN interpolation points)\n\nii.\n```python\ndef create_trials_mask(trials_df):\n    nan_exclude = ['stimOn_times', 'choice', 'feedback_times',\n                   'probabilityLeft', 'firstMovement_times', 'feedbackType']\n    mask = pd.Series(True, index=trials_df.index)\n    rt = trials_df['firstMovement_times'] - trials_df['stimOn_times']\n    if MIN_RT is not None:\n        mask &= (rt >= MIN_RT)\n    if MAX_RT is not None:\n        mask &= (rt <= MAX_RT)\n    if MAX_TRIAL_LEN is not None:\n        trial_len = trials_df['feedback_times'] - trials_df['goCue_times']\n        mask &= (trial_len <= MAX_TRIAL_LEN)\n    for event in nan_exclude:\n        if event in trials_df.columns:\n            mask &= ~trials_df[event].isna()\n    mask &= (trials_df['choice'] != 0)\n    return mask\n# Later combined:\ncombined_mask = mask.values & wheel_valid & me_valid\n```\n\niii. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Spike times and cluster IDs from multiple probes are merged (with cluster ID offsets to avoid collisions), sorted by time, then binned into 20ms time bins within each trial's time window. Binning is done by computing `floor((spike_time - trial_start) / binsize)` to get bin indices, then using `np.add.at` to accumulate spike counts per cluster per bin.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Merge probes\nmerged_times = np.concatenate(all_spike_times)\nmerged_clusters = np.concatenate(all_spike_clusters)\nsort_idx = np.argsort(merged_times, kind='stable')\n# ...\n# Bin per trial\ntime_bin_idx = np.floor((times_curr - t_beg) / BINSIZE).astype(np.int32)\ntime_bin_idx = np.clip(time_bin_idx, 0, N_BINS - 1)\nnp.add.at(binned_all[trial_idx], (clust_curr, time_bin_idx), 1)\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses `merge_probes` and `bincount2D` for equivalent operations. The AI's custom binning approach is functionally similar but uses a different algorithm (np.add.at vs bincount2D).\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The AI uses ALL clusters without QC filtering (no quality-based neuron filtering), matching the reference code's `qc=None` parameter.\n\nii.\n```python\n# In load_spikes(), all clusters from all probes are loaded without filtering\nn_clusters = len(clusters_channels)\n# No filtering based on label/quality metrics\n```\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 1 notes: \"The code loads ALL clusters (not just good ones) - qc=None.\" The reference code `prepare_data` calls `load_spiking_data(one, pid, ...)` without passing qc, which defaults to `qc=None`, including all clusters.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Trials are aligned to `stimOn_times`. The trial window is defined as `stimOn_times + (-0.5, 1.5)` seconds. For each trial, spikes within this window are binned relative to the window start.\n\nii.\n```python\nALIGN_TIME = 'stimOn_times'\nTIME_WINDOW = (-0.5, 1.5)\n# ...\nstim_on = trials_df[ALIGN_TIME].values\ntrial_starts = stim_on + TIME_WINDOW[0]\ntrial_ends = stim_on + TIME_WINDOW[1]\n```\n\niii. The task instructions say \"Temporally align based on stimulus onset\" and the reference code `0_data_caching.py` uses `align_time='stimOn_times'` with `time_window=(-0.5, 1.5)`.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The time bin size is 20ms (0.02s), producing 100 time bins per 2-second trial. No temporal rebinning is applied; this is the native binning resolution.\n\nii.\n```python\nBINSIZE = 0.02  # 20 ms\nN_BINS = int(np.ceil(INTERVAL_LEN / BINSIZE))  # 100\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses `binsize=0.02` and interval_len=2.0, producing 100 bins. The methods paper states \"divided into 20-ms bins, producing T = 100 time steps.\"\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time since stimulus onset* derived from?\n\ni. Time since stimulus onset is not derived from any raw data variable. It is computed analytically as the center of each time bin relative to stimulus onset time (which is time 0 by construction of the alignment).\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_since_stim = np.arange(N_BINS) * BINSIZE + TIME_WINDOW[0] + BINSIZE / 2\n```\n\niii. Since trials are aligned to stimOn_times, the time axis is deterministic. The values range from -0.49s to 1.49s (bin centers), with the alignment event at time 0.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time since stimulus onset*?\n\ni. A linearly spaced array of bin center times is computed. Each value equals the bin start time plus half the bin width. This array is identical for all trials.\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_since_stim = np.arange(N_BINS) * BINSIZE + TIME_WINDOW[0] + BINSIZE / 2\n# = np.arange(100) * 0.02 + (-0.5) + 0.01\n# = [-0.49, -0.47, ..., 1.49]\n```\n\niii. This is a straightforward computation. The same time array is shared across all trials as the alignment is always to stimOn_times.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time since stimulus onset* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The time since stimulus onset array and the neural data share the same 100-bin temporal structure by construction. Bin `i` of the neural data and bin `i` of the time input correspond to the same time window `[TIME_WINDOW[0] + i*BINSIZE, TIME_WINDOW[0] + (i+1)*BINSIZE]`. The input is broadcast to shape `(1, N_BINS)` for each trial.\n\nii.\n```python\ninp = np.vstack([\n    time_since_stim[np.newaxis, :],  # (1, N_BINS)\n    np.full((1, N_BINS), trial_num_in_block[i], dtype=np.float32)\n])\n```\n\niii. Since both neural and time input use the same binning structure, alignment is guaranteed.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Trial number in block* derived from?\n\ni. Trial number in block is derived from `probabilityLeft` in the trials DataFrame. Block boundaries are detected where `probabilityLeft` changes value.\n\nii.\n```python\nall_prob_left = trials_df['probabilityLeft'].values\nall_trial_nums = compute_trial_number_in_block(all_prob_left)\ntrial_num_in_block = all_trial_nums[valid_idx]\n```\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES.md notes that blocks are defined by changes in probabilityLeft (0.2, 0.5, or 0.8).\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Trial number in block*?\n\ni. The AI iterates through all trials in order. A counter starts at 1 and increments for each trial. When `probabilityLeft` changes compared to the previous trial, the counter resets to 1. The computation is done on ALL trials first (including excluded ones), then valid trial indices are selected. The value is broadcast to all time bins within a trial.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_trial_number_in_block(prob_left):\n    trial_nums = np.zeros(len(prob_left), dtype=np.float32)\n    counter = 1\n    for i in range(len(prob_left)):\n        if i > 0 and prob_left[i] != prob_left[i-1]:\n            counter = 1\n        trial_nums[i] = counter\n        counter += 1\n    return trial_nums\n```\n\niii. This approach correctly counts trials within each block and resets on block boundaries. Computing on all trials before filtering ensures the block structure is preserved correctly.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Choice* derived from?\n\ni. Choice is derived from the `choice` column of the trials DataFrame (`_ibl_trials.table.pqt`).\n\nii.\n```python\nchoice = valid_trials['choice'].values.copy()\nchoice_mapped = np.where(choice == -1, 0, 1).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The IBL convention is: choice = -1 for left, choice = 1 for right, choice = 0 for no-choice (excluded). The AI maps -1 to 0 (left) and 1 to 1 (right). No-choice trials (choice == 0) are already excluded by the trial mask. The per-trial value is broadcast to all 100 time bins.\n\nii.\n```python\nchoice_mapped = np.where(choice == -1, 0, 1).astype(np.int64)\n# ...\nnp.full((1, N_BINS), choice_mapped[i], dtype=np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The task instructions specify \"left = 0, right = 1\" which matches this encoding.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Prior probability of left* derived from?\n\ni. The reference code extracts block info from `trials_df['probabilityLeft']`.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Prior probability of left*?\n\ni. The probabilityLeft values (0.2, 0.5, or 0.8) are mapped to categorical integers: 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2. Any unexpected values default to 1 (0.5 category). The per-trial value is broadcast to all 100 time bins.\n\nii.\n```python\nprior_map = {0.2: 0, 0.5: 1, 0.8: 2}\nprior_mapped = np.array([prior_map.get(p, 1) for p in prob_left], dtype=np.int64)\n# ...\nnp.full((1, N_BINS), prior_mapped[i], dtype=np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses `SessionLoader.load_wheel()` which internally loads the same raw wheel position and timestamp arrays.\n\n## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Wheel speed*?\n\ni. The AI re-implements the wheel processing pipeline from the reference `wheel.py`:\n1. Load raw wheel position and timestamps\n2. Interpolate position to 1000 Hz uniform sampling using linear interpolation\n3. Apply Butterworth lowpass filter (order=8, corner frequency=20 Hz) using `sosfiltfilt`\n4. Compute velocity as `diff(filtered_position) * fs`, prepend a zero\n5. Speed = absolute value of velocity\n6. Per trial: interpolate the speed signal to 100 uniform bins within the trial window using linear interpolation with extrapolation at edges\n\nii.\n```python\n# Interpolate to 1000 Hz\nt_interp = np.arange(wheel_ts[0], wheel_ts[-1], 1.0 / WHEEL_FS)\npos_interp = interpolate.interp1d(wheel_ts, wheel_pos, kind='linear')(t_interp)\n# Butterworth filter and velocity\nsos = signal.butter(N=WHEEL_FILTER_ORDER, Wn=WHEEL_CORNER_FREQ / WHEEL_FS * 2,\n                    btype='lowpass', output='sos')\nvel = np.insert(np.diff(signal.sosfiltfilt(sos, pos_interp)), 0, 0) * WHEEL_FS\nspeed = np.abs(vel)\n```\n\niii. This matches the reference `interpolate_position()` and `velocity_filtered()` functions in `brainbox/behavior/wheel.py`. The reference `load_target_behavior` for 'wheel-speed' returns `np.abs(sess_loader.wheel['velocity'])`.\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Wheel speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Wheel speed is discretized into 3 bins using quantile-based binning. All speed values across all trials and timepoints are pooled, quantile boundaries at 1/3 and 2/3 are computed, and `np.digitize` is used to assign each value to bins 0, 1, or 2.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef discretize_time_varying(values, n_bins=N_DISC_BINS):\n    flat = values[~np.isnan(values)].flatten()\n    quantiles = np.linspace(0, 1, n_bins + 1)[1:-1]  # [0.333, 0.667]\n    boundaries = np.quantile(flat, quantiles)\n    result = np.digitize(values, boundaries).astype(np.int64)\n    return result\n```\n\niii. The task instructions say \"discretized into 3 bins\" but do not specify the method. Quantile-based discretization ensures approximately equal representation of each category.\n\n## 9-d. The reference code uses the same default parameters: min_rt=0.08, max_rt=2.0, max_trial_len=10.0, exclude_nochoice=True, and the same nan_exclude list.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from `spikes.times.npy` (spike timestamps) and `spikes.clusters.npy` (cluster assignments) loaded from the pykilosort spike sorting directories for each probe.\n\nii.\n```python\nspike_times = np.load(os.path.join(ks_dir, 'spikes.times.npy')).flatten()\nspike_clusters = np.load(os.path.join(ks_dir, 'spikes.clusters.npy')).flatten()\n```\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 1 documents that the reference code uses `load_spiking_data` to load spike times and clusters, which is equivalent to loading these numpy files directly.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Spike times and cluster IDs from multiple probes are merged (with cluster ID offsets to avoid collisions), sorted by time, then binned into 20ms time bins within each trial's time window. Binning is done by computing `floor((spike_time - trial_start) / binsize)` to get bin indices, then using `np.add.at` to accumulate spike counts per cluster per bin.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Merge probes\nmerged_times = np.concatenate(all_spike_times)\nmerged_clusters = np.concatenate(all_spike_clusters)\nsort_idx = np.argsort(merged_times, kind='stable')\n# ...\n# Bin per trial\ntime_bin_idx = np.floor((times_curr - t_beg) / BINSIZE).astype(np.int32)\ntime_bin_idx = np.clip(time_bin_idx, 0, N_BINS - 1)\nnp.add.at(binned_all[trial_idx], (clust_curr, time_bin_idx), 1)\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses `merge_probes` and `bincount2D` for equivalent operations. The AI's custom binning approach is functionally similar but uses a different algorithm (np.add.at vs bincount2D).\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. The AI uses ALL clusters without QC filtering (no quality-based neuron filtering), matching the reference code's `qc=None` parameter.\n\nii.\n```python\n# In load_spikes(), all clusters from all probes are loaded without filtering\nn_clusters = len(clusters_channels)\n# No filtering based on label/quality metrics\n```\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 1 notes: \"The code loads ALL clusters (not just good ones) - qc=None.\" The reference code `prepare_data` calls `load_spiking_data(one, pid, ...)` without passing qc, which defaults to `qc=None`, including all clusters.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Trials are aligned to `stimOn_times`. The trial window is defined as `stimOn_times + (-0.5, 1.5)` seconds. For each trial, spikes within this window are binned relative to the window start.\n\nii.\n```python\nALIGN_TIME = 'stimOn_times'\nTIME_WINDOW = (-0.5, 1.5)\n# ...\nstim_on = trials_df[ALIGN_TIME].values\ntrial_starts = stim_on + TIME_WINDOW[0]\ntrial_ends = stim_on + TIME_WINDOW[1]\n```\n\niii. The task instructions say \"Temporally align based on stimulus onset\" and the reference code `0_data_caching.py` uses `align_time='stimOn_times'` with `time_window=(-0.5, 1.5)`.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The time bin size is 20ms (0.02s), producing 100 time bins per 2-second trial. No temporal rebinning is applied; this is the native binning resolution.\n\nii.\n```python\nBINSIZE = 0.02  # 20 ms\nN_BINS = int(np.ceil(INTERVAL_LEN / BINSIZE))  # 100\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses `binsize=0.02` and interval_len=2.0, producing 100 bins. The methods paper states \"divided into 20-ms bins, producing T = 100 time steps.\"\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time since stimulus onset* derived from?\n\ni. Time since stimulus onset is not derived from any raw data variable. It is computed analytically as the center of each time bin relative to stimulus onset time (which is time 0 by construction of the alignment).\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_since_stim = np.arange(N_BINS) * BINSIZE + TIME_WINDOW[0] + BINSIZE / 2\n```\n\niii. Since trials are aligned to stimOn_times, the time axis is deterministic. The values range from -0.49s to 1.49s (bin centers), with the alignment event at time 0.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time since stimulus onset*?\n\ni. A linearly spaced array of bin center times is computed. Each value equals the bin start time plus half the bin width. This array is identical for all trials.\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_since_stim = np.arange(N_BINS) * BINSIZE + TIME_WINDOW[0] + BINSIZE / 2\n# = np.arange(100) * 0.02 + (-0.5) + 0.01\n# = [-0.49, -0.47, ..., 1.49]\n```\n\niii. This is a straightforward computation. The same time array is shared across all trials as the alignment is always to stimOn_times.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time since stimulus onset* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The time since stimulus onset array and the neural data share the same 100-bin temporal structure by construction. Bin `i` of the neural data and bin `i` of the time input correspond to the same time window `[TIME_WINDOW[0] + i*BINSIZE, TIME_WINDOW[0] + (i+1)*BINSIZE]`. The input is broadcast to shape `(1, N_BINS)` for each trial.\n\nii.\n```python\ninp = np.vstack([\n    time_since_stim[np.newaxis, :],  # (1, N_BINS)\n    np.full((1, N_BINS), trial_num_in_block[i], dtype=np.float32)\n])\n```\n\niii. Since both neural and time input use the same binning structure, alignment is guaranteed.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Trial number in block* derived from?\n\ni. Trial number in block is derived from `probabilityLeft` in the trials DataFrame. Block boundaries are detected where `probabilityLeft` changes value.\n\nii.\n```python\nall_prob_left = trials_df['probabilityLeft'].values\nall_trial_nums = compute_trial_number_in_block(all_prob_left)\ntrial_num_in_block = all_trial_nums[valid_idx]\n```\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES.md notes that blocks are defined by changes in probabilityLeft (0.2, 0.5, or 0.8).\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Trial number in block*?\n\ni. The AI iterates through all trials in order. A counter starts at 1 and increments for each trial. When `probabilityLeft` changes compared to the previous trial, the counter resets to 1. The computation is done on ALL trials first (including excluded ones), then valid trial indices are selected. The value is broadcast to all time bins within a trial.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_trial_number_in_block(prob_left):\n    trial_nums = np.zeros(len(prob_left), dtype=np.float32)\n    counter = 1\n    for i in range(len(prob_left)):\n        if i > 0 and prob_left[i] != prob_left[i-1]:\n            counter = 1\n        trial_nums[i] = counter\n        counter += 1\n    return trial_nums\n```\n\niii. This approach correctly counts trials within each block and resets on block boundaries. Computing on all trials before filtering ensures the block structure is preserved correctly.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Choice* derived from?\n\ni. Choice is derived from the `choice` column of the trials DataFrame (`_ibl_trials.table.pqt`).\n\nii.\n```python\nchoice = valid_trials['choice'].values.copy()\nchoice_mapped = np.where(choice == -1, 0, 1).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The reference code extracts choice directly from `trials_df['choice']` in `bin_behaviors`.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Choice*?\n\ni. The IBL convention is: choice = -1 for left, choice = 1 for right, choice = 0 for no-choice (excluded). The AI maps -1 to 0 (left) and 1 to 1 (right). No-choice trials (choice == 0) are already excluded by the trial mask. The per-trial value is broadcast to all 100 time bins.\n\nii.\n```python\nchoice_mapped = np.where(choice == -1, 0, 1).astype(np.int64)\n# ...\nnp.full((1, N_BINS), choice_mapped[i], dtype=np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The task instructions specify \"left = 0, right = 1\" which matches this encoding.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Prior probability of left* derived from?\n\ni. Prior probability of left is derived from the `probabilityLeft` column of the trials DataFrame.\n\nii.\n```python\nprob_left = valid_trials['probabilityLeft'].values\nprior_map = {0.2: 0, 0.5: 1, 0.8: 2}\nprior_mapped = np.array([prior_map.get(p, 1) for p in prob_left], dtype=np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The reference code extracts block info from `trials_df['probabilityLeft']`.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Prior probability of left*?\n\ni. The probabilityLeft values (0.2, 0.5, or 0.8) are mapped to categorical integers: 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2. Any unexpected values default to 1 (0.5 category). The per-trial value is broadcast to all 100 time bins.\n\nii.\n```python\nprior_map = {0.2: 0, 0.5: 1, 0.8: 2}\nprior_mapped = np.array([prior_map.get(p, 1) for p in prob_left], dtype=np.int64)\n# ...\nnp.full((1, N_BINS), prior_mapped[i], dtype=np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The task instructions specify \"0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2\" which matches.\n\n## 9-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Wheel speed* derived from?\n\ni. Wheel speed is derived from `_ibl_wheel.position.npy` (wheel position) and `_ibl_wheel.timestamps.npy` (wheel timestamps).\n\nii.\n```python\nwheel_pos = np.load(pos_file).flatten()\nwheel_ts = np.load(ts_file).flatten()\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses `SessionLoader.load_wheel()` which internally loads the same raw wheel position and timestamp arrays.\n\n## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Wheel speed*?\n\ni. The AI re-implements the wheel processing pipeline from the reference `wheel.py`:\n1. Load raw wheel position and timestamps\n2. Interpolate position to 1000 Hz uniform sampling using linear interpolation\n3. Apply Butterworth lowpass filter (order=8, corner frequency=20 Hz) using `sosfiltfilt`\n4. Compute velocity as `diff(filtered_position) * fs`, prepend a zero\n5. Speed = absolute value of velocity\n6. Per trial: interpolate the speed signal to 100 uniform bins within the trial window using linear interpolation with extrapolation at edges\n\nii.\n```python\n# Interpolate to 1000 Hz\nt_interp = np.arange(wheel_ts[0], wheel_ts[-1], 1.0 / WHEEL_FS)\npos_interp = interpolate.interp1d(wheel_ts, wheel_pos, kind='linear')(t_interp)\n# Butterworth filter and velocity\nsos = signal.butter(N=WHEEL_FILTER_ORDER, Wn=WHEEL_CORNER_FREQ / WHEEL_FS * 2,\n                    btype='lowpass', output='sos')\nvel = np.insert(np.diff(signal.sosfiltfilt(sos, pos_interp)), 0, 0) * WHEEL_FS\nspeed = np.abs(vel)\n```\n\niii. This matches the reference `interpolate_position()` and `velocity_filtered()` functions in `brainbox/behavior/wheel.py`. The reference `load_target_behavior` for 'wheel-speed' returns `np.abs(sess_loader.wheel['velocity'])`.\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Wheel speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Wheel speed is discretized into 3 bins using quantile-based binning. All speed values across all trials and timepoints are pooled, quantile boundaries at 1/3 and 2/3 are computed, and `np.digitize` is used to assign each value to bins 0, 1, or 2.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef discretize_time_varying(values, n_bins=N_DISC_BINS):\n    flat = values[~np.isnan(values)].flatten()\n    quantiles = np.linspace(0, 1, n_bins + 1)[1:-1]  # [0.333, 0.667]\n    boundaries = np.quantile(flat, quantiles)\n    result = np.digitize(values, boundaries).astype(np.int64)\n    return result\n```\n\niii. The task instructions say \"discretized into 3 bins\" but do not specify the method. Quantile-based discretization ensures approximately equal representation of each category.\n\n## 9-d. How is `output` *Wheel speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The continuous wheel speed signal is interpolated to 100 uniform time bins within each trial's stimulus-aligned window `(-0.5, 1.5)s`. The interpolation uses `np.linspace(t_beg, t_end, N_BINS, endpoint=False) + BINSIZE / 2` as interpolation targets (bin centers), with linear interpolation and extrapolation for edge values.\n\nii.\n```python\nx_interp = np.linspace(t_beg, t_end, N_BINS, endpoint=False) + BINSIZE / 2\ny_interp = interpolate.interp1d(\n    beh_t_clean, beh_v_clean, kind='linear',\n    fill_value='extrapolate')(x_interp)\n```\n\niii. The reference code `get_behavior_per_interval` uses `np.linspace(interval_begs + binsize, interval_ends, n_bins)` as interpolation targets (right bin edges), which is slightly different from the AI's bin centers approach.\n\n## 10-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Whisker motion energy* derived from?\n\ni. Whisker motion energy is derived from `leftCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy` (or `rightCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy` as fallback) and the corresponding camera timestamps (`_ibl_leftCamera.times.npy` or `_ibl_rightCamera.times.npy`).\n\nii.\n```python\nme_file = find_file(session_dir, 'leftCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy')\ncam_file = find_file(session_dir, '_ibl_leftCamera.times.npy')\n# ...\nme = np.load(me_file).flatten()\ncam_times = np.load(cam_file).flatten()\n```\n\niii. The reference code `bin_behaviors` loads 'whisker-motion-energy' which internally tries 'left-whisker-motion-energy' first, then 'right-whisker-motion-energy'.\n\n## 10-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Whisker motion energy*?\n\ni. The raw motion energy values are loaded directly (no additional processing of the signal itself). Per trial, the values within the trial window are interpolated to 100 uniform bins using linear interpolation.\n\nii.\n```python\n# In load_whisker_motion_energy:\nme = np.load(me_file).flatten()\ncam_times = np.load(cam_file).flatten()\n# Then in interpolate_behavior_to_bins:\ny_interp = interpolate.interp1d(beh_t_clean, beh_v_clean, kind='linear',\n    fill_value='extrapolate')(x_interp)\n```\n\niii. The reference code also loads the raw motion energy and interpolates per trial using `get_behavior_per_interval`.\n\n## 10-c. How is `output` *Whisker motion energy* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Same approach as wheel speed: quantile-based discretization into 3 bins across all trials and timepoints.\n\nii.\n```python\nme_disc = discretize_time_varying(me_data, N_DISC_BINS)\n```\n\niii. Same as wheel speed (question 9-c).\n\n## 10-d. How is `output` *Whisker motion energy* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Same approach as wheel speed: the continuous signal is interpolated to 100 uniform time bins within the stimulus-aligned trial window, using bin centers as interpolation targets.\n\nii.\n```python\nme_binned, me_valid = interpolate_behavior_to_bins(\n    me_times, me_values, trial_starts, trial_ends)\n```\n\niii. Same method as wheel speed (question 9-d).\n\n## 11. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Several missing-data scenarios are handled:\n- Sessions with missing spike data, trial data, wheel data, or motion energy data are skipped entirely\n- Trials where wheel or motion energy have fewer than 2 non-NaN interpolation points are excluded\n- NaN values in behavior signals are filtered before interpolation\n- Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are skipped\n- For `probabilityLeft` values not in {0.2, 0.5, 0.8}, the default category 1 is used\n- Trial time intervals with NaN start/end times are skipped during spike binning\n- Behavior interpolation uses `fill_value='extrapolate'` to handle edge effects\n\nii.\n```python\n# Skip sessions with missing data\nif spike_times is None:\n    return None\n# Behavior validity check\nif len(beh_v) < 2:\n    valid[trial_idx] = False\n# NaN handling in behavior\nnan_mask = ~np.isnan(beh_v)\nif nan_mask.sum() < 2:\n    valid[trial_idx] = False\n# Minimum trials\nif n_valid < 2:\n    return None\n```\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES.md documents that sessions with missing data are skipped. The reference code handles missing data through the `align_spike_behavior` function and the behavior mask system.\n\n## 12-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. Based on the timing information printed during conversion, spike binning is the most time-consuming step per session. For a session with ~1300 clusters and ~800 trials, binning takes 1.5-2.1 seconds. Wheel processing (interpolation, filtering) and behavior interpolation to trial bins are secondary bottlenecks. Total per-session time ranges from 1.3s to 4.9s.\n\nii.\n```python\nprint(f\"  Binning spikes ({n_clusters} clusters, {len(trials_df)} trials)...\", end=' ')\n# Binning takes 0.3s-2.1s per session\n```\n\niii. The conversion output shows per-session timing. Total conversion of 340 sessions took approximately 15-20 minutes.\n\n## 12-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The trial-level spike binning loop iterates over each valid trial sequentially:\n```python\nfor trial_idx in valid_indices:\n    # searchsorted + np.add.at per trial\n```\nThis could potentially be vectorized using a single bincount2D call across all trials, similar to the reference code. Additionally, the behavior interpolation loop iterates over trials:\n```python\nfor trial_idx in range(n_trials):\n    # interp1d per trial\n```\nThis is inherently hard to vectorize due to per-trial time ranges, but the reference code parallelizes it using multiprocessing.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Spike binning loop\nfor trial_idx in valid_indices:\n    i_start = np.searchsorted(spike_times, t_beg, side='left')\n    i_end = np.searchsorted(spike_times, t_end, side='left')\n    # ...\n    np.add.at(binned_all[trial_idx], (clust_curr, time_bin_idx), 1)\n\n# Behavior interpolation loop\nfor trial_idx in range(n_trials):\n    y_interp = interpolate.interp1d(...)(x_interp)\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses multiprocessing (`multiprocessing.Pool`) for both spike binning and behavior interpolation. The AI's code does not use multiprocessing.\n\n## 12-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. The wheel processing pipeline (interpolation to 1000 Hz + Butterworth filter + velocity computation) is done once per session, which is appropriate. However, the behavior interpolation to trial bins (`interpolate_behavior_to_bins`) is called separately for wheel and whisker ME, each performing a similar loop over all trials. These two calls could potentially be combined.\n\nAdditionally, the `fill_value='extrapolate'` creates a new `interp1d` object for each trial, which involves some overhead. The searchsorted operations are done independently for spike binning and for each behavior signal.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Two separate calls to interpolate_behavior_to_bins:\nwheel_binned, wheel_valid = interpolate_behavior_to_bins(\n    wheel_times, wheel_speed, trial_starts, trial_ends)\nme_binned, me_valid = interpolate_behavior_to_bins(\n    me_times, me_values, trial_starts, trial_ends)\n```\n\niii. This is not a major efficiency concern but represents a minor opportunity for optimization.\n\n## 12-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. The code bins spikes for ALL trials first (including those that will be filtered out), then applies the combined mask. This means spike binning is performed on trials that are subsequently discarded. For the first session, 692 trials are binned but only 198 survive the mask. This wastes approximately 71% of the spike binning computation for that session.\n\nSimilarly, behavior interpolation is done for all trials before filtering. Wheel and motion energy are interpolated for all trials, then valid trials are selected.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Bin ALL trials\nbinned_spikes = bin_spikes_per_trial(\n    spike_times, spike_clusters, n_clusters, trial_starts, trial_ends)\n# ...\n# Filter after binning\nvalid_idx = np.where(combined_mask)[0]\nneural_data = binned_spikes[valid_idx]\n```\n\niii. While the reference code also processes all trials before filtering (for behavior data), a more efficient approach would be to apply the trial mask before the expensive spike binning step. However, the behavior validity check requires interpolation first (to determine which trials have valid data), creating a chicken-and-egg situation that the AI resolved by processing everything first.\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}
